People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Then with those newsletter photos, I start thinking my face looks crooked, even if people tell me they don’t see it. It just kind of sticks in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your hands and face come into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yeah… they do. I don’t really want to think about it, but since I started helping with the safety trainings, I’ll notice my hands when I’m pointing, and it just… sticks. Then I start thinking about my face in those newsletter photos too, like it looks crooked, even though people say they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your appearance feel like they are forcing their way into your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes… it kind of feels that way. I’ll be doing the training and pointing at something, and then I just can’t stop noticing my hands, like they look wrinkly or uneven. Then I start thinking about the newsletter pictures too, that my face looks crooked… even when people say they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors to reduce your anxiety about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. Before a class or one of the senior-center workshops, I’ll check the bathroom mirror a few times, put lotion on my hands, turn my face different ways… sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look okay. It settles me a little, but then I’m running a bit behind. I still go, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your appearance are unreasonable?\n\nPatient: I… I know people say they don’t see it, so maybe it is a bit unreasonable. But when I’m pointing during the trainings, my hands just look so wrinkly and uneven to me. And then with the newsletter photos, I keep thinking my face looks crooked. It’s hard to shake it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. But since I started helping with the safety trainings, it keeps coming back—my hands when I’m pointing, and then those newsletter photos where my face looks crooked to me. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I’ve been getting kind of stuck on how I look, I guess. Since I started helping with safety trainings at work, I notice my hands a lot when I’m pointing—like they look wrinkly or uneven. And then in the newsletter photos, I keep thinking my face looks crooked, even though people say they don’t really see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking how your hands or face look?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do some checking. Before I teach a class or go to one of those senior-center workshops, I’ll look in the bathroom mirror a few times, put lotion on my hands, turn my face a couple ways. When I’m pointing during the safety trainings, I keep noticing they look wrinkly or uneven. Then I start thinking about the newsletter pictures too, that my face looks crooked… even when people say they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors to reduce your anxiety about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. Before a class or one of the senior-center workshops, I’ll check the bathroom mirror a few times, put lotion on my hands, turn my face different ways… sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look okay. It settles me a little, but then I’m running a bit behind. I still go, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your appearance are unreasonable?\n\nPatient: I… I know people say they don’t see it, so maybe it is a bit unreasonable. I also hesitate more before going to social things I used to just enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people can see these flaws in your appearance that you're worried about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. So part of me feels like they must notice, even if they say they don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have moments where you're able to push these thoughts about your appearance out of your mind?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… if I’m busy enough, I can push it aside for a while. Yes… that’s pretty much why I came in. Since I started helping with the safety trainings, I keep noticing my hands when I’m pointing—how wrinkly and uneven they look. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I’ve been getting kind of stuck on how I look, I guess. Since I started helping with safety trainings at work, I notice my hands a lot when I’m pointing—like they look wrinkly or uneven. And then in the newsletter photos, I keep thinking my face looks crooked, even though people say they don’t really see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking how your hands or face look?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do some checking. Before I teach a class or go to one of those senior-center workshops, I’ll look in the bathroom mirror a few times, put lotion on my hands, turn my face a couple ways. Sometimes I ask a coworker if I look okay. It can make me a little late, but I still go.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts about your appearance that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly about my hands lately. When I’m pointing during the safety trainings, I keep noticing they look wrinkly or uneven. And then with the newsletter pictures, I get stuck thinking my face looks crooked, even when other people say they don’t really see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel really upset or anxious when you think about how your hands or face look?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do get anxious about it. It’s mostly when I’m pointing during trainings and I notice my hands looking wrinkly or uneven. Then with those newsletter photos, I start thinking my face looks crooked, even if people tell me they don’t see it. It just kind of sticks in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your hands and face come into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yeah… they do. I don’t really want to think about it, but since I started helping with the safety trainings, I’ll notice my hands when I’m pointing, and it just… sticks. Then I start thinking about my face in those newsletter photos too, like it looks crooked, even though people say they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your appearance feel like they are forcing their way into your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes… it kind of feels that way. I’ll be doing the training and pointing at something, and then I just can’t stop noticing my hands, like they look wrinkly or uneven. Then I start thinking about the newsletter pictures too, that my face looks crooked… even when people say they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors to reduce your anxiety about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. Before a class or one of the senior-center workshops, I’ll check the bathroom mirror a few times, put lotion on my hands, turn my face different ways… sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look okay. It settles me a little, but then I’m running a bit behind. I still go, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your appearance are unreasonable?\n\nPatient: I… I know people say they don’t see it, so maybe it is a bit unreasonable. But when I’m pointing during the trainings, my hands just look so wrinkly and uneven to me. And then with the newsletter photos, I keep thinking my face looks crooked. It’s hard to shake it.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and worries about your appearance affecting your daily life or relationships in any significant way?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. It hasn’t really stopped me from work or volunteering… I still do those things. But I’m slower getting ready before trainings, and I’ve started avoiding photos sometimes. I also hesitate more before going to social things I used to just enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people can see these flaws in your appearance that you're worried about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. People tell me they don’t really see it… but when I’m up there pointing during the safety trainings, my hands look so wrinkly and uneven to me. And in the newsletter pictures, I keep thinking my face looks crooked. So part of me feels like they must notice, even if they say they don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have moments where you're able to push these thoughts about your appearance out of your mind?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… if I’m busy enough, I can push it aside for a while. But since I started helping with the safety trainings, it keeps coming back—my hands when I’m pointing, and then those newsletter photos where my face looks crooked to me. Even if people say they don’t see it, I still notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your appearance flaws are real, even if others can't see them?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, I do. Even when people say they don’t see it, I still notice my hands looking wrinkly and uneven when I’m pointing things out at the trainings. And then in the newsletter photos, my face just looks crooked to me. I know others don’t agree, but it feels real to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're trying to ignore these thoughts about your appearance but they just won't leave your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s pretty much why I came in. Since I started helping with the safety trainings, I keep noticing my hands when I’m pointing—how wrinkly and uneven they look. I try not to think about it, but then I see the newsletter photos and start thinking my face looks crooked, even though people say they don’t see it. It just kind of stays with me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Even if people say they don’t see it, I still notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your appearance flaws are real, even if others can't see them?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, I do. Even when people say they don’t see it, I still notice my hands looking wrinkly and uneven when I’m pointing things out at the trainings. And then in the newsletter photos, my face just looks crooked to me. I know others don’t agree, but it feels real to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're trying to ignore these thoughts about your appearance but they just won't leave your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s pretty much why I came in. Since I started helping with the safety trainings, I keep noticing my hands when I’m pointing—how wrinkly and uneven they look. I try not to think about it, but then I see the newsletter photos and start thinking my face looks crooked, even though people say they don’t see it. It just kind of stays with me.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social or occupational activities because of your concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Not really stopped me, no. I still go to work and I still help with the trainings. But… I take longer getting ready for them now, and I’ve started avoiding pictures when I can. Sometimes I hesitate before going to social things I used to just go to without thinking.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. So part of me feels like they must notice, even if they say they don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have moments where you're able to push these thoughts about your appearance out of your mind?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… if I’m busy enough, I can push it aside for a while. But since I started helping with the safety trainings, it keeps coming back—my hands when I’m pointing, and then those newsletter photos where my face looks crooked to me. Even if people say they don’t see it, I still notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your appearance flaws are real, even if others can't see them?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, I do. Even when people say they don’t see it, I still notice my hands looking wrinkly and uneven when I’m pointing things out at the trainings. And then in the newsletter photos, my face just looks crooked to me. I know others don’t agree, but it feels real to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're trying to ignore these thoughts about your appearance but they just won't leave your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s pretty much why I came in. Since I started helping with the safety trainings, I keep noticing my hands when I’m pointing—how wrinkly and uneven they look. I try not to think about it, but then I see the newsletter photos and start thinking my face looks crooked, even though people say they don’t see it. It just kind of stays with me.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social or occupational activities because of your concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Not really stopped me, no. I still go to work and I still help with the trainings. But… I take longer getting ready for them now, and I’ve started avoiding pictures when I can. Sometimes I hesitate before going to social things I used to just go to without thinking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries about your appearance are interfering with your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess. It hasn’t stopped me from working or helping with the trainings, but I’m slower getting ready now. And I’ve been avoiding photos more… sometimes I pause before going to social things I used to enjoy. So, yes, it does take something away.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I’ve been getting kind of stuck on how I look, I guess. Since I started helping with safety trainings at work, I notice my hands a lot when I’m pointing—like they look wrinkly or uneven. And then in the newsletter photos, I keep thinking my face looks crooked, even though people say they don’t really see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking how your hands or face look?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do some checking. Before I teach a class or go to one of those senior-center workshops, I’ll look in the bathroom mirror a few times, put lotion on my hands, turn my face a couple ways. Sometimes I ask a coworker if I look okay. It can make me a little late, but I still go.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts about your appearance that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly about my hands lately. When I’m pointing during the safety trainings, I keep noticing they look wrinkly or uneven. And then with the newsletter pictures, I get stuck thinking my face looks crooked, even when other people say they don’t really see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel really upset or anxious when you think about how your hands or face look?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do get anxious about it. It’s mostly when I’m pointing during trainings and I notice my hands looking wrinkly or uneven. Then with those newsletter photos, I start thinking my face looks crooked, even if people tell me they don’t see it. It just kind of sticks in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your hands and face come into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yeah… they do. I don’t really want to think about it, but since I started helping with the safety trainings, I’ll notice my hands when I’m pointing, and it just… sticks. Then I start thinking about my face in those newsletter photos too, like it looks crooked, even though people say they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your appearance feel like they are forcing their way into your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes… it kind of feels that way. I’ll be doing the training and pointing at something, and then I just can’t stop noticing my hands, like they look wrinkly or uneven. Then I start thinking about the newsletter pictures too, that my face looks crooked… even when people say they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors to reduce your anxiety about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. Before a class or one of the senior-center workshops, I’ll check the bathroom mirror a few times, put lotion on my hands, turn my face different ways… sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look okay. It settles me a little, but then I’m running a bit behind. I still go, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your appearance are unreasonable?\n\nPatient: I… I know people say they don’t see it, so maybe it is a bit unreasonable. It’s hard to shake it.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and worries about your appearance affecting your daily life or relationships in any significant way?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. It hasn’t really stopped me from work or volunteering… I still do those things. But I’m slower getting ready before trainings, and I’ve started avoiding photos sometimes. I also hesitate more before going to social things I used to just enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people can see these flaws in your appearance that you're worried about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. And in the newsletter pictures, I keep thinking my face looks crooked. So part of me feels like they must notice, even if they say they don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have moments where you're able to push these thoughts about your appearance out of your mind?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… if I’m busy enough, I can push it aside for a while.   {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I’ve been getting kind of stuck on how I look, I guess. Since I started helping with safety trainings at work, I notice my hands a lot when I’m pointing—like they look wrinkly or uneven. And then in the newsletter photos, I keep thinking my face looks crooked, even though people say they don’t really see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking how your hands or face look?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do some checking. Before I teach a class or go to one of those senior-center workshops, I’ll look in the bathroom mirror a few times, put lotion on my hands, turn my face a couple ways. Sometimes I ask a coworker if I look okay. It can make me a little late, but I still go.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts about your appearance that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly about my hands lately. When I’m pointing during the safety trainings, I keep noticing they look wrinkly or uneven. And then with the newsletter pictures, I get stuck thinking my face looks crooked, even when other people say they don’t really see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel really upset or anxious when you think about how your hands or face look?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do get anxious about it. It’s mostly when I’m pointing during trainings and I notice my hands looking wrinkly or uneven. Then with those newsletter photos, I start thinking my face looks crooked, even if people tell me they don’t see it. It just kind of sticks in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your hands and face come into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yeah… they do. I don’t really want to think about it, but since I started helping with the safety trainings, I’ll notice my hands when I’m pointing, and it just… sticks. Then I start thinking about my face in those newsletter photos too, like it looks crooked, even though people say they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your appearance feel like they are forcing their way into your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes… it kind of feels that way. I’ll be doing the training and pointing at something, and then I just can’t stop noticing my hands, like they look wrinkly or uneven. Then I start thinking about the newsletter pictures too, that my face looks crooked… even when people say they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors to reduce your anxiety about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. Before a class or one of the senior-center workshops, I’ll check the bathroom mirror a few times, put lotion on my hands, turn my face different ways… sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look okay. It settles me a little, but then I’m running a bit behind. I still go, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your appearance are unreasonable?\n\nPatient: I… I know people say they don’t see it, so maybe it is a bit unreasonable. But when I’m pointing during the trainings, my hands just look so wrinkly and uneven to me. And then with the newsletter photos, I keep thinking my face looks crooked. It’s hard to shake it.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and worries about your appearance affecting your daily life or relationships in any significant way?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. It hasn’t really stopped me from work or volunteering… I still do those things. But I’m slower getting ready before trainings, and I’ve started avoiding photos sometimes. I also hesitate more before going to social things I used to just enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people can see these flaws in your appearance that you're worried about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. People tell me they don’t really see it… but when I’m up there pointing during the safety trainings, my hands look so wrinkly and uneven to me. And in the newsletter pictures, I keep thinking my face looks crooked. So part of me feels like they must notice, even if they say they don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have moments where you're able to push these thoughts about your appearance out of your mind?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… if I’m busy enough, I can push it aside for a while. But since I started helping with the safety trainings, it keeps coming back—my hands when I’m pointing, and then those newsletter photos where my face looks crooked to me. Even if people say they don’t see it, I still notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your appearance flaws are real, even if others can't see them?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, I do. Even when people say they don’t see it, I still notice my hands looking wrinkly and uneven when I’m pointing things out at the trainings. And then in the newsletter photos, my face just looks crooked to me. I know others don’t agree, but it feels real to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're trying to ignore these thoughts about your appearance but they just won't leave your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s pretty much why I came in. Since I started helping with the safety trainings, I keep noticing my hands when I’m pointing—how wrinkly and uneven they look. I try not to think about it, but then I see the newsletter photos and start thinking my face looks crooked, even though people say they don’t see it. It just kind of stays with me.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social or occupational activities because of your concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Not really stopped me, no. I still go to work and I still help with the trainings. But… I take longer getting ready for them now, and I’ve started avoiding pictures when I can. Sometimes I hesitate before going to social things I used to just go to without thinking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries about your appearance are interfering with your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess. It hasn’t stopped me from working or helping with the trainings, but I’m slower getting ready now. And I’ve been avoiding photos more… sometimes I pause before going to social things I used to enjoy. So, yes, it does take something away.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular part of your body that you're most concerned about?\n\nPatient: Mostly my hands, I guess. When I’m pointing things out at the trainings, I notice how wrinkly and uneven they look. And then… sometimes my face too, in those newsletter photos, like it looks crooked, even though people say it doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries about your appearance are causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not terribly, but it bothers me. It hasn’t stopped me from working or volunteering, but I do take longer getting ready for trainings now, and I try to avoid photos. Sometimes I hesitate before going to social things I used to just go to. So… yes, it does upset me some.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s mostly when I’m pointing during trainings and I notice my hands looking wrinkly or uneven. I don’t really want to think about it, but since I started helping with the safety trainings, I’ll notice my hands when I’m pointing, and it just… sticks. I’ll be doing the training and pointing at something, and then I just can’t stop noticing my hands, like they look wrinkly or uneven. Then I start thinking about the newsletter pictures too, that my face looks crooked… even when people say they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors to reduce your anxiety about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. Before a class or one of the senior-center workshops, I’ll check the bathroom mirror a few times, put lotion on my hands, turn my face different ways… sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look okay. It settles me a little, but then I’m running a bit behind. I still go, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your appearance are unreasonable?\n\nPatient: I… I know people say they don’t see it, so maybe it is a bit unreasonable. It hasn’t really stopped me from work or volunteering… I still do those things. But I’m slower getting ready before trainings, and I’ve started avoiding photos sometimes. I also hesitate more before going to social things I used to just enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people can see these flaws in your appearance that you're worried about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. People tell me they don’t really see it… but when I’m up there pointing during the safety trainings, my hands look so wrinkly and uneven to me. And in the newsletter pictures, I keep thinking my face looks crooked. It hasn’t really stopped me from work or volunteering… I still do those things. But I’m slower getting ready before trainings, and I’ve started avoiding photos sometimes. I also hesitate more before going to social things I used to just enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people can see these flaws in your appearance that you're worried about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. People tell me they don’t really see it… but when I’m up there pointing during the safety trainings, my hands look so wrinkly and uneven to me. And in the newsletter pictures, I keep thinking my face looks crooked. So part of me feels like they must notice, even if they say they don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have moments where you're able to push these thoughts about your appearance out of your mind?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… if I’m busy enough, I can push it aside for a while. But since I started helping with the safety trainings, it keeps coming back—my hands when I’m pointing, and then those newsletter photos where my face looks crooked to me. Even if people say they don’t see it, I still notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your appearance flaws are real, even if others can't see them?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, I do. Even when people say they don’t see it, I still notice my hands looking wrinkly and uneven when I’m pointing things out at the trainings. And then in the newsletter photos, my face just looks crooked to me. I know others don’t agree, but it feels real to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're trying to ignore these thoughts about your appearance but they just won't leave your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s pretty much why I came in. Since I started helping with the safety trainings, I keep noticing my hands when I’m pointing—how wrinkly and uneven they look. I try not to think about it, but then I see the newsletter photos and start thinking my face looks crooked, even though people say they don’t see it. It just kind of stays with me.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social or occupational activities because of your concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Not really stopped me, no. I still go to work and I still help with the trainings. But… I take longer getting ready for them now, and I’ve started avoiding pictures when I can. Sometimes I hesitate before going to social things I used to just go to without thinking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries about your appearance are interfering with your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess.
